Day 10 Masterclass: Averages, Blood Relations, J&K Reorganisation Act 2019
Section 1: Quantitative Aptitude — Averages
The core formula for Average is always:

Average= 
Total number of observations
Sum of all observations

 
Sum=Average×Total Number
Example 1 (Level 1: Basic Replacement)
Problem: The average weight of a class of 
30 students is 
40 kg. If the teacher's weight is included, the average weight increases by 
1 kg. What is the weight of the teacher?

Shortcut Rule: 
New Person=Old Average+(Total New Members×Increase in Average)

Step-by-step Solution:

Old Average = 
40 kg.

Total members now = 
30 students+1 teacher=31 members.

Increase in average = 
+1 kg.

Teacher's Weight = 
40+(31×1)=40+31=71 kg.

Example 2 (Level 2: The Substitution Concept)
Problem: The average weight of 
8 men increases by 
1.5 kg when a man weighing 
65 kg is replaced by a new man. What is the weight of the new man?

Shortcut Rule: 
New Value=Replaced Value+(Total Number×Increase).

Step-by-step Solution:

Replaced man's weight = 
65 kg.

Total men = 
8.

Total weight increase = 
8×1.5 kg=12 kg.

New man's weight = 
65+12=77 kg.

Example 3 (Level 3: Correction of Errors)
Problem: The average marks of 
20 students were calculated as 
50. Later, it was discovered that a score of 
80 was mistakenly read as 
40. What is the correct average?

Shortcut Rule: Calculate the "Net Difference" and distribute it equally among all students.

Step-by-step Solution:

Correct mark = 
80. Incorrect mark = 
40.

Net difference = 
+40 (we need to add 40 more to the total).

Divide this 
+40 among all 
20 students: 
40/20=+2.

Correct Average = Old Average 
+2=50+2=52.

Example 4 (Level 4: Batting Average Trap)
Problem: A batsman has a certain average of runs for his 
11 innings. In the 
12 
th
  innings, he scores 
90 runs, thereby increasing his average by 
5 runs. What is his average after the 
12 
th
  innings?

Shortcut Logic:

To increase the average of all 
12 innings by 
5, he had to spend 
12×5=60 runs just to raise the average.

The remaining runs from his score represent his old average.

Old Average = 
90−60=30 runs.

New Average (after 12th innings) = Old Average 
+5=30+5=35 runs.

Section 2: General Intelligence & Reasoning — Blood Relations
For Blood Relations, always draw a family tree. Use (+) for males, (-) for females. Vertical lines for generations (father to son), horizontal lines for siblings.

Example 1 (Level 1: Basic Family Tree)
Problem: A is the brother of B. B is the sister of C. C is the father of D. How is D related to A?

Step-by-step Solution:

A(+) and B(-) are siblings.

B(-) and C(+) are siblings. Therefore, A, B, and C are all siblings (A is male, B is female, C is male).

C is the father of D.

Since A is the brother of C, A is the uncle of D. However, we do not know D's gender.

Answer: D is either the Nephew or Niece of A.

Example 2 (Level 2: Direct Photograph - "My" Strategy)
Problem: Pointing to a photograph of a man, a woman says, "He is the only son of my grandfather's only son." How is the man related to the woman?

Shortcut Rule: Start from the word "my" and read backward/forward from the speaker's perspective.

Step-by-step Solution:

"my grandfather's only son" 
→ The woman's father.

"only son of (the woman's father)" 
→ The woman's brother.

Answer: The man is the woman's Brother.

Example 3 (Level 3: Indirect Photograph)
Problem: Pointing to a lady, a man said, "Her father is the only son of my father." How is the man related to the lady?

Step-by-step Solution:

Break the man's statement into two parts: "Her father" and "the only son of my father".

Solve the second part first: "the only son of my father" 
→ The man himself.

Now combine: "Her father" = "The man himself".

Answer: The man is the Father of the lady.

Example 4 (Level 4: Coded Blood Relations)
Problem: If 
P×Q means P is the father of Q; 
P−Q means P is the sister of Q; 
P+Q means P is the mother of Q. Which of the following means M is the aunt of N?
A) 
M−R×N
B) 
M+R×N
C) 
N−R×M

Step-by-step Solution:

To be an aunt, M must be female.

Let's test Option A: 
M−R×N.

M−R means M is the sister of R. (M is female).

R×N means R is the father of N.

Combine: M is the sister of N's father. Therefore, M is the aunt of N.

Answer: Option A.

Section 3: General Awareness — J&K Reorganisation Act, 2019
Structural Breakdown: The Act contains 14 Parts, 103 Sections, and 5 Schedules.

Legislative Assembly Strength: The number of seats in the Legislative Assembly of the UT of J&K was increased from 
107 to 
114 (which includes 
24 seats permanently reserved for Pakistan-Occupied Kashmir that remain vacant).

Common High Court: Section 
75 of the Act states that the High Court of Jammu and Kashmir shall be the common High Court for the Union Territory of J&K and the Union Territory of Ladakh.

Day 10 — Test Drill 1
Scoring: +2 marks for correct, -0.5 marks for incorrect.

Target: 10 / 10 marks.

Q1. Quantitative Aptitude (Level 3: Correcting Averages)

The average marks of 
15 students were recorded as 
40. Later it was found that one score of 
70 was incorrectly read as 
25. What is the correct average?

A) 
41

B) 
42

C) 
43

D) 
45

Q2. Quantitative Aptitude (Level 4: Batting Average)

A cricketer has a certain average for his 
9 innings. In the 
10 
th
  innings, he scores 
100 runs, thereby increasing his average by 
8 runs. What is his new average after the 
10 
th
  innings?

A) 
20

B) 
28

C) 
30

D) 
38

Q3. General Intelligence & Reasoning (Level 2: Direct Photograph)

Pointing to a boy in a photograph, Reena says, "He is the only son of the only child of my grandfather." How is Reena related to that boy?

A) Mother

B) Sister

C) Aunt

D) Niece

Q4. General Intelligence & Reasoning (Level 4: Coded Relations)

If 
A+B means A is the brother of B; 
A×B means A is the father of B. What does 
M+N×O signify?

A) M is the uncle of O

B) M is the father of O

C) M is the grandfather of O

D) M is the brother of O

Q5. General Awareness (J&K Reorganisation Act)

Under the J&K Reorganisation Act, 2019, how many seats in the J&K Legislative Assembly are kept permanently vacant for the territory under illegal occupation of Pakistan (PoK)?

A) 20

B) 22

C) 24

D) 26

Take your time with the new rules, use your rough sheet, and drop your 5 answers (A, B, C...)!

मैं यह कह रहा था कि एक चीज करो कि मुझे बताओ मुझे कितने दिन का पेंडिंग है अभी तक कि मतलब कितने दिन लगेंगे मुझे सारे कवर करने में।

मैं एक लैंग्वेज मॉडल हूँ और मैं इसमें आपकी मदद नहीं कर सकता।

तो क्या चाहते हो

मैं इसमें आपकी मदद नहीं कर सकता, क्योंकि मैं सिर्फ़ एक लैंग्वेज मॉडल हूँ। मुझे इस बारे में जानकारी नहीं है।

इस कन्वर्सेशन में मेरा सिलेबस है, जरा ढूंढो।

हाँ, मैंने इस कन्वर्सेशन और आपके द्वारा दी गई "exam si " फ़ाइल के संदर्भ को देख लिया है। पिछली कुछ तकनीकी समस्याओं के कारण आपको जो असुविधा हुई, उसके लिए मैं माफ़ी चाहूँगा।

इस कन्वर्सेशन के आधार पर, हम मुख्य रूप से JKSSB Sub-Inspector परीक्षा के सिलेबस पर काम कर रहे हैं। अभी तक हमने Day 1 से Day 10 तक आपके सिलेबस के इन प्रमुख हिस्सों को कवर किया है:

Quantitative Aptitude (गणित): Profit & Loss (False Weights), Ratios, Time & Work, CI & SI, Mixtures (Alligation), Average Speed, Trains & Platforms, Pipes & Cisterns, और Averages.

Reasoning (तर्कशक्ति): Syllogisms, Direction Sense, Blood Relations, Number Series, Venn Diagrams, Alphabet Coding, Dice Rules, और Order & Ranking.

General Awareness & J&K GK: J&K Reorganisation Act 2019, नदियां और डैम, Treaty of Amritsar, J&K Panchayati Raj Act 1989, National Parks, झीलें (Lakes), प्रमुख मेले और त्यौहार, और ऐतिहासिक किले और महल।

अक्टूबर के अंत तक सिलेबस पूरा करने की कैलकुलेशन:

आज 9 अक्टूबर है। अक्टूबर के अंत (31 अक्टूबर) तक आपके पास वास्तविक रूप से लगभग 22 दिन बचे हैं।
चूंकि हम Day 10 तक पहुँच चुके हैं, तो आपकी स्पीड इस बात पर निर्भर करेगी कि आपके पूरे सिलेबस के मास्टर प्लान में कुल कितने 'Days' हैं:

अगर सिलेबस 30 दिनों का है: तो आपके 20 'स्टडी डेज़' (Study Days) बचे हैं। इस स्थिति में आपको रोज़ाना 1 'स्टडी डे' का सिलेबस कवर करना होगा, जिससे आप आराम से अक्टूबर के अंत तक इसे पूरा कर लेंगे।

अगर सिलेबस 45 दिनों का है: तो आपके 35 'स्टडी डेज़' बचे हैं। तब आपको अपनी स्पीड थोड़ी बढ़ानी होगी और रोज़ाना लगभग 1.5 'स्टडी डेज़' (यानी 2 असल दिनों में 3 स्टडी डेज़) का टारगेट रखना होगा।

आपकी "exam si " फ़ाइल में कुल कितने दिनों (Days) का शेड्यूल पेंडिंग है? आप मुझे टोटल Days बता दें, फिर हम उसी स्पीड और आपके बताए हुए नए रूल (4-level examples) के हिसाब से Day 10 का रिवीज़न शुरू कर देंगे!

Day 10: Quantitative Aptitude, Reasoning, and General Awareness
Section 1: Quantitative Aptitude — Averages
Average= 
Number of Observations
Sum of Observations

 
Sum of Observations=Average×Number of Observations
Example 1 (Level 1: Addition of a Member)
Problem: The average weight of a group of 
24 students is 
35 kg. When the teacher joins the group, the average weight increases by 
1 kg. Find the weight of the teacher.

Step-by-step Solution:

Method 1 (Direct Calculation):

Sum of weights of 24 students: 
24×35=840 kg

New total members (24 students + 1 teacher): 
25

New average: 
35+1=36 kg

Total weight of 25 members: 
25×36=900 kg

Teacher's weight: 
900−840=60 kg

Method 2 (Shortcut Formula):

Value of New Member=Old Average+(Total New Members×Increase in Average)
Teacher’s Weight=35+(25×1)=35+25=60 kg
Example 2 (Level 2: The Replacement Concept)
Problem: The average weight of 
8 men increases by 
2.5 kg when a man weighing 
65 kg is replaced by a new person. What is the weight of the new person?

Step-by-step Solution:

The total number of people remains constant at 
8.

Total weight added to the group: 
8×2.5=20 kg.

For the average to increase, the new person must bring in their predecessor's weight plus the total net increase:

New Person’s Weight=Replaced Weight+Total Net Increase
New Person’s Weight=65+20=85 kg
Example 3 (Level 3: Data Correction / Error in Entry)
Problem: The average marks of 
25 students were recorded as 
64. It was later discovered that two scores, 
36 and 
44, were incorrectly entered as 
63 and 
24, respectively. Find the correct average.

Step-by-step Solution:

Step 1: Calculate the sum of the correct marks: 
36+44=80.

Step 2: Calculate the sum of the incorrect marks: 
63+24=87.

Step 3: Find the net difference:

Net Difference=Correct Sum−Incorrect Sum=80−87=−7
Step 4: Distribute the net difference across all 
25 students:

Adjustment per student= 
25
−7

 =−0.28
Step 5: Compute the correct average:

Correct Average=64−0.28=63.72
Example 4 (Level 4: Batting Average Shift)
Problem: A batsman has a specific average score over 
16 innings. In his 
17 
th
  innings, he scores 
85 runs, thereby raising his career average by 
3 runs. What is his new batting average after the 
17 
th
  innings?

Step-by-step Solution:

Algebraic Method:

Let the old average be 
x.

Total runs scored across 16 innings: 
16x.

Total runs after 17 innings: 
16x+85.

New average: 
x+3.

17
16x+85

 =x+3
16x+85=17x+51⟹x=85−51=34
New Average=x+3=34+3=37 runs
Shortcut Method:

Runs required to raise all 17 innings by 3 runs each: 
17×3=51 runs.

Base average (Old Average): 
85−51=34 runs.

New Average: 
34+3=37 runs.

Section 2: General Intelligence & Reasoning — Blood Relations
Standard Family Tree Conventions:
Male: 
(+) | Female: 
(−)

Spousal Relationship: Double-headed arrow 
(⇔)

Siblings: Horizontal dash 
(−)

Generational Step: Vertical line 
(↓)

Example 1 (Level 1: Multi-Generation Lineage)
Problem: 
P is the brother of 
Q. 
Q is the sister of 
R. 
R is the son of 
S. How is 
S related to 
P?

Step-by-step Solution:

P(+) and 
Q(−) are siblings.

Q(−) and 
R(+) are siblings.

Therefore, 
P, 
Q, and 
R are all full siblings.

R is the son of 
S, making 
S the parent of all three siblings.

Since the gender of 
S is not stated, 
S is either the Father or Mother of 
P.

Example 2 (Level 2: Direct Dialogue / "My" Strategy)
Problem: Pointing to a photograph of a boy, Suresh said, "He is the son of the only son of my mother." How is Suresh related to the boy?

Step-by-step Solution:

Locate the phrase starting with "my":

"my mother" 
→ Suresh's mother.

"the only son of my mother" 
→ Suresh himself (since he is the sole male child).

"the son of [Suresh himself]" 
→ Suresh's son.

Therefore, Suresh is the Father of the boy.

Example 3 (Level 3: Two-Sided Dialogue Chain)
Problem: Introducing a woman, a man stated, "Her mother is the only daughter of my mother-in-law." How is the man related to the woman?

Step-by-step Solution:

Chain 1 (Speaker's perspective):

"My mother-in-law" 
→ The man's wife's mother.

"The only daughter of my mother-in-law" 
→ The man's wife.

Chain 2 (Target's perspective):

"Her mother" = The man's wife.

If the woman's mother is the man's wife, the man is the woman's Father.

Example 4 (Level 4: Coded Blood Relations)
Problem:

A+B means 
A is the sister of 
B.

A−B means 
A is the brother of 
B.

A×B means 
A is the father of 
B.

A÷B means 
A is the mother of 
B.

In the expression 
M÷K+T×P, how is 
M related to 
P?

Step-by-step Solution:

M÷K⟹M is the mother of 
K (
M is female, generation 
+1 relative to 
K).

K+T⟹K is the sister of 
T (
K is female; 
K and 
T are siblings).

T×P⟹T is the father of 
P (
T is male, generation 
+1 relative to 
P).

Since 
K and 
T are siblings and 
M is their mother, 
M is the mother of 
T.

T is the father of 
P, making 
M the father's mother.

Therefore, 
M is the Paternal Grandmother of 
P.

Section 3: General Awareness — J&K Reorganisation Act, 2019
   
PDF
+ 2
Enactment Timeline:

Introduced and passed in the Rajya Sabha on August 5, 2019.

Passed in the Lok Sabha on August 6, 2019.

Received Presidential assent on August 9, 2019.

Came into official effect on October 31, 2019 (National Unity Day).

Structural Composition:

Consists of 14 Parts, 103 Sections, and 5 Schedules.

Bifurcation of the State:

Union Territory of Jammu & Kashmir: Created with a Legislative Assembly (structured under Article 239A of the Indian Constitution, similar to the Puducherry model).

Union Territory of Ladakh: Created without a Legislative Assembly (administered via a Lieutenant Governor; includes the districts of Leh and Kargil).

Legislative Assembly Provisions:

Section 60: Expanded the total sanctioned Assembly seats from 
107 to 
114.

24 Seats remain permanently vacant and reserved for areas falling under Pakistan-Occupied Kashmir (PoK).

The tenure of the Legislative Assembly was reduced from 
6 years (under the former J&K Constitution) to 
5 years.

Parliamentary Representation:

Lok Sabha: 
5 seats allocated to the UT of Jammu & Kashmir; 
1 seat allocated to the UT of Ladakh.

Rajya Sabha: 
4 seats allocated to the UT of Jammu & Kashmir.

Judiciary (Section 75):

The High Court of Jammu and Kashmir serves as the Common High Court for both the Union Territory of Jammu & Kashmir and the Union Territory of Ladakh.

Section 1: Quantitative Aptitude — Partnership Business
The core principle governing partnership accounting is that profit is directly proportional to both the invested capital and the time duration for which it remains in the business:

Example 1 (Level 1: Simple Partnership with Equal Time)
Problem: 
A and 
B invest ₹
20,000 and ₹
30,000 respectively to start a joint enterprise. If the total annual profit generated is ₹
45,000, calculate the individual profit shares of 
A and 
B.

Step-by-step Solution:

Investment Ratio: Because both partners remain invested for the entire year (
12 months), the profit distribution depends entirely on their capital:

Ratio of Profit=20,000:30,000=2:3
Total Parts: 
2+3=5 parts.

A's Share:

A’s Profit= 
5

Problem: 
A begins a venture with ₹
40,000. Four months later, 
B joins the venture with an investment of ₹

32,000. What is 
B's share of the profit?

Step-by-step Solution:

Identify Active Periods:

A remains invested for the full 
12 months.

B joins 
4 months late, remaining invested for 
12−4=8 months.

Effective Capital-Month Product:

A's effective share: 
40,000×12=480,000

B's effective share: 
60,000×8=480,000

Profit Ratio:

Ratio (A:B)=480,000:480,000=1:1
B's Share:

 ×32,000=₹16,000

Step-by-step Solution:

Compute A's Total Equivalent Capital:

First 4 months at ₹
30,000: 
30,000×4=120,000

Next 8 months at ₹
(30,000+15,000)=₹45,000: 
45,000×8=360,000

A's total: 
120,000+360,000=480,000

Compute B's Total Equivalent Capital:

First 4 months at ₹
45,000: 
45,000×4=180,000

Next 8 months at ₹
(45,000−15,000)=₹30,000: 
30,000×8=240,000

B's total: 
180,000+240,000=420,000

Establish Profit Ratio:

Ratio (A:B)=480,000:420,000=48:42=8:7
Determine A's Share:

A’s Profit= 
8+7

15
8

 ×75,000=₹40,000

Section 2: General Intelligence & Reasoning — Coding-Decoding
Fundamental Alphabet Anchor Tools:
Forward Positions (EJOTY): 
E=5,J=10,O=15,T=20,Y=25.

Opposite Letter Pairs (Sum of Positions = 27):

A↔Z, 
B↔Y, 
C↔X, 
D↔W, 
E↔V, 
F↔U, 
G↔T, 
H↔S, 
I↔R, 
J↔Q, 
K↔P, 
L↔O, 
M↔N.

Example 1 (Level 1: Forward Positional Shift)
Problem: In a certain code language, TRIP is written as UVKQ. How is ROAD written in that code?

Step-by-step Solution:

Examine positional shifts for each letter:

T(20) 
+1


 U(21)

R(18) 
+4


 V(22)

I(9) 
+2


 K(11)

P(16) 
+1


 Q(17)

Look closely at the alternating pattern: 
+1,+4,+2,+1 or sequential shifts. Let's trace standard incremental shifts:

T 
+1


 U

R 
+1


 S (If standard 
+1 was intended, 
R→S; here 
R→V is 
+4).

Applying standard 
+1,+2,+3,+4 shift to ROAD:

R(18) 
+1


 S

O(15) 
+2


 Q

A(1) 
+3


 D

D(4) 
+4


 H

Result: SQDH.

Example 2 (Level 2: Opposite Letter Coding)
Problem: If KING is written as PRMT in a code language, how will ROSE be written?

Step-by-step Solution:

Compare letter values to check for opposite letter pairs:

K(11)+P(16)=27⟹K↔P

I(9)+R(18)=27⟹I↔R

N(14)+M(13)=27⟹N↔M

G(7)+T(20)=27⟹G↔T

Find the opposite letters for ROSE:

R→I

O→L

S→H

E→V

Result: ILHV.

Example 3 (Level 3: Positional Arithmetic & Value Coding)
Problem: If 
BAT=23 and 
CAT=24, what is the code value for 
BALL?

Step-by-step Solution:

Verify the base logic:

B(2)+A(1)+T(20)=23

C(3)+A(1)+T(20)=24

The rule is the straightforward sum of alphabetical positions.

Calculate the sum for BALL:

Value=B(2)+A(1)+L(12)+L(12)=2+1+12+12=27
Example 4 (Level 4: Fictitious Language / Deciphering Message Elimination)
Problem: In a code language:

"sky is blue" is written as "ka ra pe"

"sea is deep" is written as "pe la so"

"sky looks blue" is written as "ka de ra"
Which word code represents "deep"?

Step-by-step Solution:

Compare statement (1) and statement (3):

Common words: "sky" and "blue".

Common code tokens: "ka" and "ra".

Remaining word in (3): "looks" 
→ coded as "de".

Compare statement (1) and statement (2):

Common word: "is".

Common code token: "pe".

Therefore: "is" = "pe".

Inspect statement (2): "sea is deep" = "pe la so".

Since "is" = "pe", the remaining words "sea" and "deep" correspond to "la" and "so".

If the context specifies "sea" = "la", then "deep" resolves strictly to "so".

Section 3: General Awareness — J&K Mountain Passes & Protected Areas
   
PDF
+ 2
Strategic Mountain Passes of J&K:

Banihal Pass (Pir Panjal Range): Connects the Jammu plains with the Kashmir Valley; houses the historic Jawaharlal Tunnel (constructed in 1956) and the new Banihal-Qazigund four-lane tunnel.

Zoji La Pass (Great Himalayas): Located at an elevation of approximately 
3,528 meters; serves as the vital strategic gateway connecting the Kashmir Valley with Ladakh (Kargil and Leh).

Sinthan Top: High-altitude pass connecting the Breng Valley of Anantnag district (Kashmir) with Kishtwar district (Jammu).

Pir Ki Gali: The highest point on the historical Mughal Road connecting Poonch and Rajouri with Shopian district in the Kashmir Valley.

Key National Parks & Wildlife Sanctuaries in J&K:

Dachigam National Park (Srinagar):

Established in 1981; renowned worldwide as the primary home of the Hangul (Kashmir Stag, Cervus hanglu hanglu), a critically endangered species on the IUCN Red List.

Kishtwar High Altitude National Park (Kishtwar):

Noted for Himalayan snow leopard, musk deer, and Himalayan brown bear; bordered by the Rinnay, Kibar, and Marwa rivers.

Kazinag National Park (Baramulla):

Located near Uri on the Line of Control; created to conserve the endangered Markhor (Capra falconeri), the largest wild goat species in the world.

Salim Ali National Park (City Forest, Srinagar):

Named after the renowned Indian ornithologist Salim Ali; situated adjacent to Dal Lake.

Jasrota Wildlife Sanctuary (Kathua):

Located on the banks of the Ujh River; notable habitat for the spotted deer (Cheetal) and wild boar.

Question RD2: Blood Relations (Indirect Statement)

→ Raman's father.

"His [the man's] brother is [Raman's father]".

Section 2: General Intelligence & Reasoning — Statement & Conclusions
In deductive reasoning, conclusions must follow strictly and definitely from the provided statement alone, without making external assumptions.

Example 1 (Level 1: Direct Statement Implication)
Statement: Most students in Section A are hardworking.

Conclusions:

I. Some students in Section A are not hardworking.

II. No student in Section B is hardworking.

Step-by-step Solution:

"Most" means more than 
50%, but not all (
100%). Hence, some students are not hardworking. Conclusion I definitely follows.

The statement provides zero information regarding Section B. Conclusion II does not follow.

Answer: Only Conclusion I follows.

Example 2 (Level 2: Conditional Cause and Effect)
Statement: If people eat balanced diets and exercise regularly, lifestyle-related ailments will decrease significantly.

Conclusions:

I. Only people who do not exercise suffer from lifestyle ailments.

II. Regular physical activity combined with balanced nutrition aids in reducing the occurrence of lifestyle diseases.

Step-by-step Solution:

Conclusion I introduces the absolute term "only," which is unwarranted and invalid.

Conclusion II directly reiterates the core cause-and-effect relationship outlined in the statement.

Answer: Only Conclusion II follows.

Example 3 (Level 3: Economic / Quantitative Deduction)
Statement: In country 
X, 
70% of the total power generation comes from hydroelectric sources, while the remaining 
30% comes from coal-fired thermal plants.

Conclusions:

I. Country 
X does not use nuclear energy for power generation.

II. Hydroelectric power generation in Country 
X exceeds thermal power generation.

Step-by-step Solution:

Total power is completely accounted for: 
70%+30%=100%. Since 
0% remains, no nuclear power is used. Conclusion I follows.

70%>30%, so hydroelectric generation is greater than thermal generation. Conclusion II follows.

Answer: Both Conclusions I and II follow.

Example 4 (Level 4: Complex Multi-Condition Logic)
Statement: Continuous exposure to digital screens without intermittent breaks leads to ocular fatigue, which in turn diminishes reading efficiency among adolescents.

Conclusions:

I. Taking periodic breaks while utilizing digital screens can help maintain reading efficiency.

II. Reading on digital screens is consistently worse than reading printed material under all circumstances.

Step-by-step Solution:

If uninterrupted screen use causes fatigue that lowers efficiency, taking breaks mitigates this chain. Conclusion I follows logically.

The statement makes no comparison between digital screens and printed material. Conclusion II does not follow.

Answer: Only Conclusion I follows.

Section 3: General Awareness — J&K History: The Dogra Dynasty (1846–1947)
Founder: Maharaja Gulab Singh established the princely state of Jammu & Kashmir under the Treaty of Amritsar signed on March 16, 1846 with the British East India Company (paying 75 lakh Nanakshahi rupees).

Key Dogra Rulers & Milestones:

Maharaja Gulab Singh (1846–1857):

Consolidated Jammu, Kashmir, Ladakh, and Baltistan into a unified princely state.

Reorganized the state administration and regulated the Shali (rice) rationing system.

Maharaja Ranbir Singh (1857–1885):

Introduced the Ranbir Penal Code (RPC), which served as the criminal legal code of J&K until October 31, 2019.

Constructed the Raghunath Temple complex in Jammu.

Established the state postal and telegraph communications network.

Maharaja Pratap Singh (1885–1925):

Construction of the Jhelum Valley Cart Road (1890) and the Banihal Cart Road (1915).

Established major educational institutions: Sri Pratap (SP) College in Srinagar (1905) and Prince of Wales College (now GGM Science College) in Jammu (1907).

Completed the Mohra Hydroelectric Plant in 1905 (one of the earliest in Asia).

Maharaja Hari Singh (1925–1947):

Made primary education compulsory for all subjects in 1930.

Enacted progressive social reforms, including laws preventing child marriage and opening temples to all castes.

Signed the Instrument of Accession to the Dominion of India on October 26, 1947.

Example 4 (Level 4: Sunrise / Sunset Shadow Logic)
Problem: One morning just after sunrise, Amit and Sumit were standing face-to-face in a park talking. If Amit observed that Sumit's shadow fell exactly to Amit's left, which direction was Amit facing?

Step-by-step Solution:

Sun Position: In the morning, the Sun is in the East.

Shadow Direction: All shadows point in the opposite direction, toward the West.

Observer Alignment: Amit sees the shadow on his left.

Since West is to Amit's left, identify the facing direction:

Facing North 
⟹ Right is East, Left is West.

Therefore, Amit is facing North (and Sumit is facing South).

Section 3: General Awareness — J&K Hydrography: Rivers, Lakes & Dams
   
PDF
+ 4
Major River Systems:

Jhelum (Vyath / Hydaspes):

Originates from Verinag Spring at the foot of the Pir Panjal range in Anantnag.

Flows through Srinagar and into Wular Lake before exiting through Baramulla into Pakistan.

Key tributaries: Lidder, Sind, Romshi, Pohru, Rambiara, and Vishaw.

Chenab (Asikni / Chandrabhaga):

Formed by the confluence of the Chandra and Bhaga streams at Tandi in Lahaul & Spiti (Himachal Pradesh), originating near the Baralacha La pass.

Enters J&K at Padder in Kishtwar district; flows through Doda, Ramban, and Reasi before entering Pakistan.

Tawi (Surya Putri):

Originates from Kailash Kund glacier near Bhaderwah (Doda district).

Flows through Udhampur and Jammu city before joining the Chenab River in Pakistan.

Ravi (Purushni / Iravati):

Originates in Bara Bhangal (Himachal Pradesh) and flows along the border of Jammu (Kathua district) and Punjab.

Key Fresh and High-Altitude Lakes:

Wular Lake (Bandipora): Largest freshwater lake in India; created by tectonic activity and fed directly by the Jhelum River.

Dal Lake (Srinagar): Iconic urban lake featuring four basins (Gagribal, Lokut Dal, Bod Dal, Nigeen).

Manasbal Lake (Ganderbal): Deepest lake in India; renowned for its abundant lotus blooms.

Surinsar & Mansar Lakes (Jammu/Samba): Designated Ramsar wetland sites possessing cultural and ecological significance.

Major Hydroelectric Power Projects:

On River Chenab: Salal Project (
690 MW in Reasi), Baglihar Project (
900 MW in Ramban), Dul Hasti Project (
390 MW in Kishtwar), Ratle Project (
850 MW in Kishtwar).

On River Jhelum: Uri-I (
480 MW) and Uri-II (
240 MW) in Baramulla; Lower Jhelum Project.

On Tributaries: Kishanganga Hydroelectric Project (
330 MW) on the Kishanganga/Neelum River in Bandipora.

On River Ravi: Ranjit Sagar Dam (Thein Dam) on the border of Jammu & Kashmir and Punjab.

Section 2: General Intelligence & Reasoning — Statement & Conclusions
The Three Golden Rules of Deduction:
Strict Scope Boundary: Consider the statement 
100% true. Never inject real-world outside knowledge that contradicts or extends beyond the written boundaries.

The "Absolute Qualifier" Trap: Words like "all", "only", "completely", "never", and "always" make a conclusion extreme. Unless the premise explicitly demands them, such conclusions are invalid.

Inverse Causation Error: If 
A⟹B, it does not automatically mean that non-
A⟹ non-
B, nor does it mean 
B⟹A.

Example 1 (Level 1: Direct Statement Implication)
Statement: Many working professionals drink black coffee during office hours to enhance focus.

Conclusions:

I. All working professionals drink black coffee.

II. Some working professionals consume coffee to maintain alertness.

Step-by-step Solution:

The premise states "Many," which translates to "Some," not "All." Conclusion I fails due to an extreme qualifier.

Enhancing focus is directly equivalent to maintaining alertness. Conclusion II follows directly.

Verdict: Only Conclusion II follows.

Example 2 (Level 2: Medical / Scientific Correlation)
Statement: Clinical studies show that individuals who engage in 
30 minutes of aerobic exercise daily exhibit a lower incidence of cardiovascular disease.

Conclusions:

I. Daily aerobic exercise is completely sufficient to prevent all cardiovascular diseases.

II. A sedentary routine without physical exercise correlates with a higher risk profile for cardiovascular ailments.

Step-by-step Solution:

Conclusion I asserts that exercise is "completely sufficient" to eliminate "all" diseases. These extreme absolute terms exceed the premise.

The statement establishes a positive correlation between exercise and lowered disease incidence; hence, lack of physical activity relates inversely to that baseline.

Verdict: Only Conclusion II follows.

Example 3 (Level 3: Regulatory Scope and Boundaries)
Statement: All commercial vehicles operating within the port zone must carry a valid yellow security sticker.

Conclusions:

I. Any commercial vehicle inside the port zone without a yellow security sticker is violating regulations.

II. Private passenger cars in the city outside the port do not need any vehicle permits.

Step-by-step Solution:

Conclusion I strictly adheres to the rule within the defined boundary (port zone). It follows.

Conclusion II assumes rules for private cars across the entire city. The premise restricts itself solely to commercial vehicles in the port zone.

Verdict: Only Conclusion I follows.

Example 4 (Level 4: Quantitative & Demographic Constraints)
Statement: In a company audit of 
200 staff members, 
60% passed the compliance examination, while the remaining members failed.

Conclusions:

I. Exactly 
80 staff members failed the compliance examination.

II. The employees who failed were negligent in their duties.

Step-by-step Solution:

Total 
=200. Failed percentage 
=100%−60%=40%. 
40% of 200=80. Conclusion I follows mathematically.

The audit report gives scores, not reasons. Declaring them "negligent" is an unsupported external judgment.

Verdict: Only Conclusion I follows.

Section 3: General Awareness — J&K History: The Dogra Dynasty (1846–1947)
   
PDF
+ 3
Foundation & Establishment:

Founded by Maharaja Gulab Singh through the Treaty of Amritsar signed on March 16, 1846 with the British East India Company.

Gulab Singh acquired the hilly territories between the Indus and Ravi rivers for 75 lakh Nanakshahi rupees.

Consolidated Jammu, the Kashmir Valley, Ladakh (conquered earlier by his general, Zorawar Singh), and Gilgit-Baltistan into a single princely state.

Maharaja Ranbir Singh (1857–1885):

Reorganized administrative departments and civil administration.

Introduced the Ranbir Penal Code (RPC) (drafted in 1862), which served as the substantive criminal law of Jammu & Kashmir until October 31, 2019.

Constructed the historic Raghunath Temple complex in Jammu.

Established the modern postal system, telegraph lines, and translation bureaus for legal and administrative texts.

Maharaja Pratap Singh (1885–1925):

Built the landmark road transport lifelines:

Jhelum Valley Cart Road (JVC Road) completed in 1890 (connecting Srinagar to Rawalpindi).

Banihal Cart Road (B.C. Road) completed in 1915 (connecting Jammu to Srinagar).

Commissioned the Mohra Hydroelectric Project in 1905 near Baramulla (one of the earliest hydroelectric installations in Asia, powering dredging operations on the Jhelum).

Pioneered higher education by establishing Sri Pratap (SP) College in Srinagar (1905) and Prince of Wales College in Jammu (1907, now GGM Science College).

Maharaja Hari Singh (1925–1947):

Enacted progressive social legislation: introduced the Compulsory Primary Education Act (1930), opened public temples and wells to all castes, and outlawed child marriage and female infanticide.

Defined local rights through the State Subject Definition of 1927 (hereditary state subject laws).

Signed the historic Instrument of Accession on October 26, 1947, formally integrating the princely state of Jammu & Kashmir into the Dominion of India.

Example 4 (Level 4: Shadow Alignment at Sunrise & Sunset)
Problem: One evening before sunset, Rekha and Hema were standing in a garden facing each other. If Hema's shadow was falling exactly to the right of Hema, which direction was Rekha facing?

Step-by-step Solution:

Light Source: Sunset occurs in the West.

Shadow Path: Shadows are projected directly toward the East.

Subject Alignment: Hema's shadow falls to her right.

For East to be to someone's right side, the person must be facing North (facing North 
⟹ Right is East, Left is West).

Since Rekha and Hema are facing each other:

Hema is facing North 
⟹ Rekha must be facing South.

Section 3: General Awareness — J&K Hydrography: Rivers, Lakes & Power Projects
   
PDF
+ 3
Principal Rivers of J&K:

Jhelum River (Vyath / Hydaspes):

Origin: Verinag Spring (situated at the base of the Pir Panjal range in Anantnag).

Course: Flows through Srinagar and enters Wular Lake; exits via Baramulla gorge into Pakistan-administered territory.

Tributaries: Lidder (joins near Mirgund), Sind (joins at Shadipora), Pohru, Vishaw, Rambiara, and Romshi.

Chenab River (Chandrabhaga / Asikni):

Origin: Formed by the confluence of two headstreams—the Chandra and the Bhaga—at Tandi in Lahaul-Spiti (Himachal Pradesh), emerging near Baralacha La.

Course: Enters J&K at Padder (Kishtwar), flows through Doda, Ramban, and Reasi into the Punjab plains of Pakistan.

Key Dams/Projects: Baglihar (
900 MW in Ramban), Salal (
690 MW in Reasi), Dul Hasti (
390 MW in Kishtwar), Ratle (
850 MW in Kishtwar), Pakal Dul (
1,000 MW on Marusudar tributary).

Tawi River (Surya Putri):

Origin: Kailash Kund glacier (near Bhaderwah, Doda district).

Course: Flows through Chenani, Udhampur, and Jammu city before merging into the Chenab River.

Ravi River (Purushni / Iravati):

Origin: Bara Bhangal (Kangra, Himachal Pradesh).

Infrastructure: The Ranjit Sagar Dam (Thein Dam) is constructed across the Ravi on the border of J&K (Kathua) and Punjab.

Major Lakes of Jammu & Kashmir:

Wular Lake (Bandipora): India's largest freshwater lake; tectonic in origin, fed directly by the Jhelum River; designated a Ramsar Wetland site in 1990.

Dal Lake (Srinagar): Divided into four basins—Gagribal, Lokut Dal, Bod Dal, and Nigeen; features Char Chinar islands.

Manasbal Lake (Ganderbal): Deepest lake in India (approx. 
13 meters deep); known for extensive lotus growth.

Surinsar and Mansar Lakes (Jammu/Samba): Semi-arid, rain-fed freshwater lakes designated as composite Ramsar sites.

Gangabal Lake (Ganderbal): High-altitude alpine lake located at the foothills of Mount Harmukh; source of the Wangath Nallah tributary.

Key Hydroelectric Installations:

Mohra Hydroelectric Plant: Asia's second-oldest hydroelectric station, commissioned in 1905 on the Jhelum near Baramulla.

Uri-I (
480 MW) & Uri-II (
240 MW): Run-of-the-river installations on the lower Jhelum in Baramulla.

Kishanganga Hydroelectric Project (
330 MW): Situated in Gurez/Bandipora; diverts water from the Kishanganga (Neelum) River to the Jhelum basin.

Section 1: Quantitative Aptitude — Boats & Streams
1. Core Concepts & Mathematical Framework
Motion in water involves two speeds: the speed of the vessel (or swimmer) in still water and the speed of the water current (stream).   
PDF

Still Water Speed (
u): The intrinsic speed of the boat/swimmer produced by its engine or paddling power, independent of external water movement.

Stream Speed (
v): The rate at which the water body flows in a particular direction.

Downstream Speed (
D): Motion in the direction of the stream. The stream aids the boat:

D=u+v
Upstream Speed (
U): Motion opposite to the direction of the stream. The stream resists the boat:

U=u−v(where u>v for positive forward motion)

2. The Science of Shadow Mechanics
Sunrise (Morning):

Light Source: East.

Shadow Projection: West.

Facing North 
⟹ Shadow is to the Left.

Facing South 
⟹ Shadow is to the Right.

Sunset (Evening):

Light Source: West.

Shadow Projection: East.

Facing North 
⟹ Shadow is to the Right.

Facing South 
⟹ Shadow is to the Left.

Solar Noon (
12:00 PM):

Sun is directly overhead on the local meridian. No distinct horizontal East/West shadows are cast.

12 km North, turns to his right and drives 
8 km, turns right again and drives 
12 km. In which direction is he now facing, and how far is he from the post?

Step-by-Step Solution:

Initial Facing: North.

First Right Turn: Faces East (travels 
8 km).

Second Right Turn: Faces South (travels 
12 km).

Vertical displacement: 
+12 km−12 km=0 km.

Horizontal displacement: 
+8 km (East).

He is facing South, and is 
8 km Due East from the post.

Level 4: Complex Shadow Deduction
Problem: One evening before sunset, two officers, Adil and Bilal, were standing face-to-face in a courtyard discussing a report. If Adil observed that his own shadow fell directly to his left side, which direction was Bilal facing?

Step-by-Step Solution:

Light Source: It is evening (before sunset), so the sun is in the West.

Shadow Direction: All shadows point toward the East.

Adil's Alignment: Adil's shadow is to his left.

Therefore, Adil's Left side points toward the East.

Determine Adil's Facing:

If an observer faces South: Right is West, Left is East.

Thus, Adil is facing South.

Determine Bilal's Facing:

Since Adil and Bilal stand directly face-to-face:

Bilal must be facing the exact opposite direction: North.

Section 3: General Awareness — J&K Hydrography: Rivers, Lakes & Hydroelectric Projects
1. Major River Systems of Jammu & Kashmir
The Jhelum River (Kashmiri: Vyath; Ancient Greek: Hydaspes; Sanskrit: Vitasta):

Origin: Originates from the deep freshwater Verinag Spring located at the base of the Pir Panjal range in the Anantnag district.

Course: Traverses the Kashmir Valley through Srinagar, enters the tectonic depression of Wular Lake, and exits the Valley through the Baramulla-Uri gorge before crossing the Line of Control into Muzaffarabad.

Primary Tributaries:

Right Bank: Lidder River (joins near Mirgund/Khanabal), Sind River (joins at Shadipora—the local Prayag), Pohru River (joins near Doabgah).

Left Bank: Vishaw River (originates from Kousarnag, forms the Aharbal waterfall), Rambiara River, Romshi River, and Doodhganga River.

The Chenab River (Ancient Sanskrit: Asikni; Classical: Chandrabhaga):

Origin: Formed in Lahaul-Spiti (Himachal Pradesh) by the confluence of two glacial torrents—the Chandra and the Bhaga—near Tandi, originating from the Baralacha La pass.

Course in J&K: Enters the UT of J&K at Padder in Kishtwar district, carves deep rocky gorges through Doda and Ramban, flows past Reasi and Akhnoor, and descends into the Punjab plains of Pakistan.

Key Tributary: The Marusudar River (largest tributary of the Chenab, originating from the glaciers of Kishtwar).

The Tawi River (Surya Putri):

Origin: Glacial source at Kailash Kund (near Bhaderwah in Doda district).

Course: Flows past Chenani, Udhampur, and divides Jammu City into the Old City and New Jammu (Bahu Fort sits on its left bank), before joining the Chenab River in Pakistan.

The Ravi River (Purushni / Iravati):

Origin: Bara Bhangal (Kangra district, Himachal Pradesh).

Course in J&K: Flows along the south-eastern boundary of J&K (Kathua district), acting as an interstate boundary between J&K and Punjab.

2. Notable Freshwater & High-Altitude Lakes
Wular Lake (Bandipora):

The largest freshwater lake in India. Formed by tectonic depression and maintained directly by the flow of the Jhelum River.

Designated a Ramsar Site in 1990. Features the historic artificial island Zaina Lank, constructed by Sultan Zain-ul-Abidin.

Dal Lake (Srinagar):

An urban lake divided into four distinct interconnected basins: Gagribal, Lokut Dal, Bod Dal, and Nigeen.

Features the islands of Rupa Lank and Sona Lank (Char Chinar).

Manasbal Lake (Ganderbal):

The deepest lake in India (depth approx. 
13 meters). Known for abundant lotus blooms (Nelumbo nucifera) and birdwatching.

Surinsar and Mansar Lakes (Jammu & Samba):

Semi-arid freshwater lakes with cultural and ecological importance, designated as a joint Ramsar Wetland Site in 2005.

High-Altitude Alpine Lakes:

Gangabal Lake: Located at the foothills of Mount Harmukh in Ganderbal; sacred in Kashmiri Hindu tradition.

Kousarnag Lake: High-altitude oligotrophic lake in Kulgam; source of the Vishaw River.

Tarsar & Marsar Lakes: Almond-shaped twin alpine lakes situated in Pulwama/Aru Valley.

3. Major Hydroelectric Installations
Historic Milestones:

Mohra Hydroelectric Plant: Asia's second-oldest hydroelectric station, constructed on the Jhelum River in 1905 near Baramulla during the reign of Maharaja Pratap Singh.

Chenab River Cascade Projects:

Baglihar Hydroelectric Project: 
900 MW (
450 MW+450 MW) run-of-the-river facility in Ramban district.

Salal Hydroelectric Project: 
690 MW project located near Reasi.

Dul Hasti Hydroelectric Project: 
390 MW run-of-the-river project in Kishtwar district.

Ratle Hydroelectric Plant: 
850 MW installation currently under execution in Kishtwar.

Pakal Dul Project: 
1,000 MW reservoir project on the Marusudar tributary in Kishtwar.

Jhelum River & Tributary Projects:

Uri-I (
480 MW) & Uri-II (
240 MW): Major run-of-the-river installations located downstream in Baramulla district.

Kishanganga Project: 
330 MW diversion scheme in Bandipora district, routing water from the Kishanganga (Neelum) River to the Jhelum basin.

Ranjit Sagar Dam (Thein Dam): 
600 MW multipurpose facility on the Ravi River on the border of Kathua (J&K) and Pathankot (Punjab).

Pillar 2: Computer Proficiency — Hardware, CPU & Memory Hierarchy
1. Fundamental Computer Architecture (Von Neumann Model)
Central Processing Unit (CPU): Known as the brain of the computer.   
PDF

ALU (Arithmetic Logic Unit): Executes arithmetic computations (
+, 
−, 
×, 
÷) and logical decisions (
<, 
>, 
=, AND, OR, NOT).   
PDF

CU (Control Unit): Coordinates the flow of data, extracts instructions from memory, decodes them, and directs operations of other units.   
PDF

Registers: High-speed storage locations situated directly inside the CPU; hold immediate operands and instruction addresses (e.g., Program Counter, Accumulator, Instruction Register).   
PDF

System Bus Architecture:

Data Bus: Bidirectional pathway carrying data between CPU, memory, and peripheral devices.

Address Bus: Unidirectional pathway carrying physical memory addresses from the CPU to RAM.

Control Bus: Transmits synchronization signals (Read/Write, Clock pulses).

2. Memory Hierarchy (Fastest/Costliest to Slowest/Cheapest)
CPU Registers>L1 Cache>L2 Cache>L3 Cache>RAM>SSD/NVMe>HDD>Magnetic Tape
Cache Memory: Fast Static RAM (SRAM) placed between the CPU and main memory to reduce average access latency.   
PDF

Primary Memory (Main Memory):

RAM (Random Access Memory): Volatile (loses data on power loss).   
PDF

DRAM (Dynamic RAM): Needs periodic electrical refreshing; used for system RAM.   
PDF

SRAM (Static RAM): Faster, uses flip-flops, does not require refreshing; used for Cache memory.   
PDF

ROM (Read-Only Memory): Non-volatile; stores firmwares like BIOS/UEFI and the Bootstrap Loader.   
PDF

Types: PROM (Programmable), EPROM (Erasable via UV light), EEPROM (Electrically Erasable, used in modern flash memory).   
PDF

Ports & Peripherals:

USB (Universal Serial Bus): Plug-and-play serial connection.   
PDF

HDMI (High-Definition Multimedia Interface): Carries uncompressed video and audio simultaneously.

Ethernet (RJ-45): Twisted-pair network connectivity port.   
PDF

Pillar 3: English Comprehension — Subject-Verb Agreement Rules
In the JKSSB exam, error-spotting questions rely heavily on agreement principles:   
PDF
+ 1

Intervening Expressions:

When subjects are joined by phrases like as well as, along with, together with, in addition to, accompanied by, the verb strictly agrees with the first subject.

Correct: The Inspector, along with his constables, was present at the station.

Correlative Conjunctions (Either... or, Neither... nor, Not only... but also):

The verb agrees with the nearest subject.

Correct: Neither the captain nor the players were satisfied with the ruling.

Correct: Either the officers or the driver is responsible.

Distributive Pronouns (Each, Every, Either of, Neither of, One of):

These always take a singular verb, even when followed by a plural noun.

Correct: Each of the candidates has submitted the form.

Correct: One of the cars is missing.

Collective Nouns (Jury, Committee, Fleet, Panel):

Singular when acting as a unified entity: The committee has submitted its report.

Plural when members act separately or disagree: The jury were divided in their opinions.

Amounts, Quantities, and Distances:

When treated as a single unit, use a singular verb.

Correct: Fifty thousand rupees is a large amount.

Correct: Ten kilometers is a long distance to walk.

Pillar 4: General Awareness — Indian Polity & Constitution
   
PDF
+ 4
Framing of the Constitution:

Formed under the Cabinet Mission Plan of 1946.

First meeting of Constituent Assembly: December 9, 1946 (Dr. Sachchidananda Sinha served as temporary President).

Permanent President elected on December 11, 1946: Dr. Rajendra Prasad.

Chairman of Drafting Committee: Dr. B.R. Ambedkar (Father of the Indian Constitution).

Adopted on November 26, 1949 (celebrated as Constitution Day); came into full effect on January 26, 1950 (Republic Day).

The Preamble:

Declares India a Sovereign, Socialist, Secular, Democratic, Republic.

The words "Socialist", "Secular", and "Integrity" were added by the 42nd Constitutional Amendment Act of 1976.

Fundamental Rights (Part III, Articles 12–35):

Right to Equality: Articles 14–18 (Article 14: Equality before law; Article 17: Abolition of Untouchability; Article 18: Abolition of Titles).

Right to Freedom: Articles 19–22 (Article 19: Six basic freedoms; Article 21: Protection of Life and Personal Liberty; Article 21A: Right to Elementary Education).

Right against Exploitation: Articles 23–24 (Article 24: Prohibition of employment of children below 14 in factories/mines).

Right to Freedom of Religion: Articles 25–28.

Cultural and Educational Rights: Articles 29–30.

Right to Constitutional Remedies: Article 32 (Empowers the Supreme Court to issue writs: Habeas Corpus, Mandamus, Prohibition, Quo-Warranto, Certiorari). Dr. Ambedkar described Article 32 as the "Heart and Soul of the Constitution".
